@@132376636 <h> OPINION <h> SA 's crushing burden -- the NDR <p> William Saunderson-Meyer <p> 12 December 2025 <p> William Saunderson-Meyer says the movement 's fixation on the revolution continue to sabotage its prospects for success <p> JAUNDICED EYE <p> Rapprochement with Israel and a reset of South Africa 's fraught diplomatic relationship with the United States may be on the cards following the ANC 's National General Council ( NGC ) policy summit this week . <p> It 's the clearest indication that pressure from the Trump administration is biting . Tariffs , aid cuts and the threat of exclusion , from the Africa Growth and Opportunities Act are doing what years of pleading by local business , the DA , liberal think tanks , and Afrikaner pressure groups could not : nudging the ANC away from its radical posturing and embrace of rogue regimes towards genuine non-alignment . <p> In its 72-page ' base document ' to delegates , the ANC proposes that on the Israel-Palestine question , South Africa should back a two-state solution -- at times the party has echoed Hamas 's ' from the river to @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ to Gaza , ' while engaging all sides diplomatically ' . <p> If this approach survives the NGC debates and , as crucially , is not sabotaged by the Islamist faction that is so influential in the Department of International Relations and Cooperation ( DIRCO ) , it would be a sea change . It would open the door to reversing a decade of downgrades in relations with Israel and restoring full diplomatic representation between the two countries . Those ties were progressively hollowed out from 2018 , when Pretoria recalled its ambassador from Tel Aviv and later refused to replace him . The rift widened in 2023 when South Africa pulled its remaining diplomats , and Israel , in turn , recalled its ambassador from Pretoria . <p> Such a shift will meet stiff resistance from ANC radicals . The party has cultivated warm ties with Hamas , and support for the BDS ( Boycott , Divestment and Sanctions ) campaign is official ANC policy . It has cheered BDS in international forums and backed local boycott drives , from campaigns against Jewish-owned and Israel-linked retailers to the University @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , which imposed a de facto academic boycott by in effect barring UCT academics from working with Israeli scholars . <p> --&gt; <p> On the broader foreign-policy front , the base document is equally startling . While the ANC should ' continue to stand in solidarity with just causes worldwide ' , it must also be ' pragmatic ' about South Africa 's ' core interests in a tough global environment ' . It warns that ' trade relations with the West are still significant ' and that we share ' much common ground with the EU . <p> ' We refuse to be bullied into choosing camps , ' the document states , ' but we also should avoid reckless provocation . A principle of " friends with all , enemies with none " , as long as our core values are respected , should guide us . ' <p> One wonders how the ANC will square all this with its support for Al Jama-ah -- backed by the EFF , MKP , Rise Mzansi , GOOD , the PAC and the NCP -- in introducing an ' Apartheid @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ 1973 UN Apartheid Convention and the Rome Statute , creating a stand-alone crime of ' apartheid ' and ' apartheid state ' , and giving South African courts extraterritorial jurisdiction over such offences . In plain language , it is an attempt by pro-Palestinian activists to provide a South African legal basis for boycotts , divestment and sanctions against Israel , and to prosecute those who support or defend it . <p> If the NGC proposals are implemented in good faith -- signalled by the ANC withdrawing support for the Apartheid Bill -- these policy recommendations would amount to a major reset with the US , with the prospect of salvaging crucial trade benefits that President Donald Trump 's administration has put at risk . It is also an implicit admission that the message from Washington has never been quite as coercive as the ANC 's angry rhetoric about the subversion of South African sovereignty has asserted . <p> --&gt; <p> One wonders how the ANC will square all this with its stated intention to support Al Jama-ah , backed by the EFF , MKP , Rise Mzansi , GOOD @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ introduce an ' Apartheid Bill ' . If passed , it would domesticate the 1973 UN Apartheid Convention and the Rome Statute , creating a stand-alone crime of ' apartheid ' and ' apartheid state ' , and giving South African courts exterritorial jurisdiction over such crimes . In other words , it 's an attempt by pro-Palestinian activists to provide a South African legal basis for boycotts , divestment and sanctions against Israel , and to prosecute those who support or defend Israel . <p> If the NGC proposals are implemented in good faith -- which would also mean the ANC withdrawing support for the Apartheid Bill -- these policy recommendations would amount to a major reset with the US , with the prospect of salvaging the crucial trade and aid benefits that President Donald Trump 's administration paused . It is also an implicit admission that the message from Washington has never been quite as coercive as the ANC 's angry rhetoric about the subversion of South African sovereignty has asserted . <p> While the US would obviously prefer a docile client state , the realpolitik is that @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ What it has signalled repeatedly -- both in unacknowledged direct engagements with Pretoria and through back-channels via business leaders and groups like Solidarity and AfriForum -- is that it can live with honest non-alignment , but will exact a very high price if Pretoria persists with its provocatively anti-Western , pro-rogue-state line . <p> If only the same hard-headed realism that is beginning to creep into ANC foreign policy could influence its domestic thinking . This potential shift in our international relations is one of the few unambiguously positive proposals put before the 1,650 party delegates cloistered for four days in Boksburg to engage in ' self-reflection , correction and renewal ' . <p> --&gt; <p> Of course , one should welcome any effort to renew , rewire and repurpose a dysfunctional political party whose policies are so deeply destructive . One should , however , be sceptical about the chances of tarting up something that endlessly promises reform -- it has had more ' renewal ' campaigns than Joan Rivers has had face-lifts -- without moving beyond cosmetics to the deep surgery needed . <p> It likely will be @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ some genuine attempts to come to terms with the stark reality of the 2024 general election . Over the 15 years from 2009 , the ANC 's share of the vote has slid from 66% to 40% , and its support has fallen from 11.5m voters to about 6.5m -- roughly five million people who have turned their back on the party , albeit not by flocking to the opposition but by quietly walking off the field . <p> The base document duly lists all the obvious crises : poverty , inequality and unemployment , corruption , violent crime , an attempted insurrection , social decay , local-government collapse , a faltering state , economic stagnation and skittish investors . President Cyril Ramaphosa 's report to the NGC wrapped this in the language of ' renewal ' , calling for ethical leadership , tougher action against the corrupt , and a ' capable , developmental state ' . He urged delegates to ' go back to basics ' and conceded that the ANC had lost public confidence and could no longer shelter behind liberation-era glories . <p> But in the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ 's aspirations could only be realised through the National Democratic Revolution ( NDR ) , which requires nothing more fundamental than a ' refreshed vision ' . His overview and the base document alike reaffirmed the NDR as the ' central objective ' of the movement , and framed the exercise as the rescue of a revolutionary project that has gone off the rails , not its replacement . <p> --&gt; <p> And here , in a nutshell , is the problem , the NDR elephant in the policy room . The ANC says it wants a ' developmental state ' in the mould of the Asian tigers . Its own base document points admiringly to Vietnam , China and Singapore as models of ruthless anti-corruption drives and state-led , high-growth development . But those states , whatever their revolutionary origins , long ago stopped worshipping at the altar of a permanent revolution . They are hard-nosed , export-driven nations that prize competence , high-level education , stability and capital formation over cadre deployment and racial point-scoring . <p> The NDR is a massive obstacle to South Africa 's dreams @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ ability to deliver it . Its tenets colour and cloud the ANC 's every response to the world . <p> When opposition parties and national interest groups lobby against ANC diktats on BEE , expropriation or race-based laws and regulations , they are not engaged in the quotidian activities that characterise a constitutional democracy . Instead , the base document identifies this as the malignant ' confluence of forces that seek to derail the NDR and inflict lasting and irreparable damage on the ANC and the revolutionary Alliance , the historic locomotive of progressive transformation in our country ' . <p> This is messianic and delusional stuff . Even while admitting ANC failings , the base documents insist that these failings are not the ANC 's fault but the result of malicious external forces that deliberately ' sabotage the ANC 's governance capability -- hollow out state institutions and SOEs through deployment of unethical or incapable individuals , foster corruption and patronage networks -- and thereby cripple service delivery and development capacity . This ... sought to make the ANC fail in government , so as to discredit it among @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ 's recommendation is more of the same -- the same old NDR elephant , now with Asian-style ' go-faster ' tiger stripes painted on its hide . What is needed , according to the base document , is wider BEE regulations that are more strictly policed ; higher taxation and wealth taxes ; vigorous land and asset redistribution ; and ' reforming the financial sector to align credit and investment with national development goals ' . <p> The message , should those corporate leaders who fawn over the ANC have the wit to read it , is clear . Despite all the talk of social compacts with business and strategic public-private partnerships , there is no disguising the ANC 's visceral hostility to the private sector . Everything has to be state-led , and that state , even in the GNU , must be driven by the ANC 's NDR . <p> One of the most chilling passages is that dealing with unemployed youth . The ANC proposes ' volunteer corps for unemployed youth ( who can then be integrated into ANC programmes or public employment projects ) to give @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ educate them so they are not swayed by anti-ANC rhetoric ' . In plain English , that is state-funded work gangs with ANC political instructors -- a sanitised , 21st-century echo of Soviet-style youth brigades a hundred years ago . <p> Staring into the electoral abyss has forced the ANC , however reluctantly , towards rethinking its foreign policy . But on the domestic stage , it remains a Marxist-Leninist ideologue , combining a romantic na ? vet ? with brutal anti-democratic instincts . Until the NDR is ditched , every promised ' renewal ' will be just nip , tuck and filler on the ugly face of a failing regime . <p> This is the last Jaundiced Eye column for 2024 . The next is Saturday , January 10 , 2026 . @@132422376 <h> Why Marwan Barghouti 's name matters <p> Graffiti for the freedom of Marwan Barghouti , a jailed political activist from Fatah . ( Flickr ) <p> When Hamas demanded the release of Marwan Barghouti , many asked a simple question : why him ? To understand the answer , one must understand Barghouti 's unique position in Palestinian political life -- and why he is often described as the most popular leader of his generation . <h> A lifetime of political activism <p> Barghouti , a senior figure in Fatah , became politically active as a teenager . His early involvement in student movements in the West Bank and Gaza led to his first arrest in 1978 . During his imprisonment , he learned Hebrew and completed his secondary schooling . After his release , he returned to the West Bank and pursued a degree in history and political science . <h> Role in the First Intifada <p> By 1987 , Palestinian communities faced growing economic hardship and a rapid expansion of Israeli settlements in the West Bank . Tensions erupted into the First Intifada after an Israeli military @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ refugee camp , killing four workers . <p> Protests spread rapidly , and Barghouti emerged as a key organiser . He was arrested and later exiled to Jordan . <p> Throughout this period , Israel faced international criticism for the use of excessive force . Amnesty International documented cases of unlawful killings at demonstrations and checkpoints . Palestinians , for the most part , were unarmed , relying on mass civil protest that occasionally involved stone-throwing . The uprising lasted six years . <h> The Oslo years and rising influence <p> Barghouti returned to the West Bank in 1994 following the signing of the Oslo Accords between Israel and the Palestine Liberation Organisation ( PLO ) . The agreements recognised the PLO as the legitimate representative of the Palestinian people and acknowledged Israel 's right to exist . In theory , they were meant to establish a five-year interim framework leading to peace . <p> The Palestinian Authority ( PA ) was created to administer parts of the occupied territories . Barghouti rose quickly within Fatah , becoming its general secretary in 1998 . <p> It was also during this @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the Oslo framework . International law prioritises the rights of people , not the permanence of political structures . States gain legitimacy through protecting human rights , not through asserting metaphysical rights to exist . <p> This tension -- between a process centred on state recognition and a reality defined by ongoing occupation -- shaped Barghouti 's growing political influence . <h> The Second Intifada and imprisonment <p> As it became increasingly clear that Israel was not meeting key obligations under the Oslo framework , Barghouti advocated for both negotiations and popular resistance . When the Second Intifada erupted in 2000 , he was viewed as one of the uprising 's central political figures . <p> Israel attempted to assassinate him and , in 2002 , arrested him in Ramallah . An Israeli court later convicted him of involvement in attacks that killed 26 people and sentenced him to five consecutive life terms . Barghouti denied targeting civilians but expressed support for armed resistance against military occupation -- a form of resistance recognised under international law . <p> Even behind bars , Barghouti 's influence only grew . He was @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ in 2017 , led a mass 43-day hunger strike calling attention to the conditions of Palestinian prisoners . <h> October 7 and the present moment <p> Since 7 October , reports from human rights organisations indicate that Palestinian detainees -- including Barghouti -- have faced severe mistreatment . Despite this , his stature has not diminished . Instead , calls for his release have intensified , especially as Palestinian politics edge closer to potential elections . <p> Barghouti is widely expected to run for the presidency . Polls over the past decade consistently show him as the most popular political figure among Palestinians , viewed by many as capable of uniting a deeply fragmented political landscape . <h> Why his release matters <p> Hamas 's demand for Barghouti 's release reflects his symbolic and political weight . For many Palestinians , he represents a bridge between factions , generations and political visions . <p> For Israel , however , his popularity and legitimacy make him a strategic challenge . <p> Barghouti 's fate -- whether he remains in prison or is released through negotiations -- is now entangled with the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ ? zarn Barday is a South African lawyer . She writes on human rights , international law , and political developments in the Middle East . The views expressed are her own

@@132458010 <h> Arms sales and revenue surging worldwide -- SIPRI research <p> The Lockheed Martin and Rheinmetall partnered global mobile artillery rocket system ( GMARS ) live fired for the first time at White Sands Missile Range , New Mexico , August 2025 . Photo : US Army , via SIPRI . <p> Fuelled by , among others , wars in Gaza and Ukraine as well as rising global and regional geopolitical tensions , 2024 saw arms and military service sales by the 100 largest defence companies rise by just short of six percent to a record high of $679 billion . <p> The numbers are contained in data released on 1 December by the Stockholm International Peace Research Institute ( SIPRI ) which calls the revenue increase for arms companies a sharp rise . It notes further all five of the largest arms companies -- Lockheed Martin , RTX ( formerly Raytheon Technologies ) , Northrop Grumman , BAE Systems and the General Dynamics Corporation -- increased their arms revenues . <p> A SIPRI statement has it the bulk of the global rise was due to companies based in Europe @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ in all world regions featured in the Top 100 . The only exception was Asia and Oceania , where issues in the Chinese arms industry drove down the regional total . The revenue " surge " added to new orders prompted many arms companies to expand production lines , enlarge facilities , establish new subsidiaries or conduct acquisitions . <p> " Last year global arms revenues reached the highest level ever recorded by SIPRI as producers capitalized on high demand , " said Lorenzo Scarazzato , Researcher with the SIPRI Military Expenditure and Arms Production Programme . " Although companies have been building their production capacity , they still face a range of challenges that could affect costs and delivery schedules . " <p> Last year saw the combined arms revenues of United States ( US ) arms companies in the Top 100 grow by 3.8 % to reach $334 billion , with 30 out of the 39 US companies in the ranking increasing their arms revenues . These included major arms producers Lockheed Martin , Northrop Grumman and General Dynamics . <p> Widespread delays and budget overruns continue to @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the F-35 combat aircraft , the Columbia-class submarine and the Sentinel intercontinental ballistic missile ( ICBM ) , SIPRI researchers found . Several of the largest US arms producers are affected by overruns , raising uncertainty about when major new weapon systems and upgrades to existing ones can be delivered and deployed . <p> " The delays and rising costs will inevitably impact US military planning and military spending , " said Xiao Liang , Researcher with the SIPRI Military Expenditure and Arms Production Programme . " This could have knock-on effects on the US government 's efforts to cut excessive military spending and improve budget efficiency . " <p> Of the 26 arms companies in the Top 100 based in Europe ( excluding Russia ) , 23 recorded increasing arms revenues . Aggregate arms revenues grew by 13% to $151 billion . This increase was tied to demand stemming from the war in Ukraine and the perceived threat from Russia . <p> The Czechoslovak Group recorded the sharpest percentage increase in arms revenues of any Top 100 company in 2024 -- 193% -- to reach $3.6 billion . The @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Czechoslovak Group benefited from the Czech Ammunition Initiative , a government-led project to source artillery shells for Ukraine . Ukraine 's own JSC Ukrainian Defence Industry increased arms revenues by 41% to $3billion . <p> " European arms companies are investing in new production capacity to meet the rising demand , " said Jade Guiberteau Ricard , Researcher with the SIPRI Military Expenditure and Arms Production Programme . " But sourcing materials could pose a growing challenge . In particular , dependence on critical minerals is likely to complicate European rearmament plans . " <p> As an example of the critical minerals risk dependence , the statement names trans-European company Airbus and France 's Safran , both of which met half their pre-2022 titanium needs with Russian imports and have had to find new suppliers . Further , in the light of Chinese export restrictions on critical minerals , companies including Thales ( France ) and Rheinmetall ( Germany ) warned of potential high costs of restructuring supply chains . <p> The two Russian arms companies in the Top 100 , Rostec and United Shipbuilding Corporation , increased combined arms @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ leading to a component shortage . Domestic demand was sufficient to more than offset revenue lost due to falling arms exports . <p> " Besides sanctions , Russian arms companies are facing a shortage of skilled labour . This could slow production and limit innovation , " said Diego Lopes da Silva , Senior Researcher with the SIPRI Military Expenditure and Arms Production Programme . " However , we need to be cautious making such predictions , as Russia 's arms industry has proved resilient during the war in Ukraine , contrary to expectations . " <p> The Asia and Oceania region was the only one in the world to record an overall decline in arms revenues among Top 100 companies in 2024 , falling to $130 billion , 1.2% down on 2023 . The picture , SIPRI Military Expenditure and Arms Production Programme Director Nan Tian said , was highly varied in the region . It is ascribed to a combined 10% decline in arms revenues among the eight Chinese arms companies in the Top 100 . Most prominent was the 31% drop in arms revenue by Norinco , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ A host of corruption allegations in Chinese arms procurement led to major arms contracts being postponed or cancelled in 2024 , " said Nan Tian , Director of the SIPRI Military Expenditure and Arms Production Programme . " This deepens uncertainty around the status of China 's military modernization efforts and when new capabilities will materialize . The five Japanese companies increased combined arms revenues by 40% to $13.3 billion , with the four South Korean producers increasing arms revenues by 31% to $14.1 billion . @@132524174 <p> When the Hague Group convened its first public African meeting in Cape Town earlier this week , it did so as a coalition of Global South states seeking to revive the enforcement of international law in the face of Israel 's genocide on Gaza . <p> Co-chaired by South Africa and Colombia , the group brings together states from Africa , Latin America and Asia that argue the paralysis of multilateral institutions has enabled systematic impunity . Yet the gathering unfolded against a backdrop that exposed a central tension confronting the initiative . <p> It was here that former international relations minister Naledi Pandor 's intervention cut most sharply . Welcoming the formation of the group , Pandor cautioned that without confronting material complicity , the initiative risked becoming " pleasant , but ineffectual " . <p> Her warning would soon find concrete expression in trade data showing a sharp rise in South African coal exports to Israel , even as Pretoria presses its genocide case at The Hague . <p> The Hague Group was established in January by South Africa , Colombia , Bolivia , Cuba , Honduras @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ . <p> Drawn from three continents with histories shaped by colonial domination , the group has committed to implementing existing international law through domestic measures , including halting arms transfers , denying port access for weapons shipments and enforcing arrest warrants against individuals accused of war crimes should they enter member states ' territories . <p> That ambition reflects mounting frustration with the failure of global institutions to constrain Israeli conduct in Gaza . Speakers described a system in which legal norms are applied selectively , allowing powerful states and their allies to evade accountability . <p> South Africa 's case against Israel at the International Court of Justice was repeatedly cited as a turning point that challenged this exceptionalism , even as subsequent diplomatic pressure against Pretoria underscored the costs of acting alone . <p> From the outset , organisers framed the Hague Group as an attempt to overcome that vulnerability through collective action . By coordinating enforcement , they argue , states may generate enough political ballast to resist unilateral retaliation , including sanctions , tariffs and diplomatic isolation . Several participating countries have already faced punitive measures @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ urgency to the experiment . <p> But as discussion in Cape Town made clear , collective credibility depends on consistency . During the question-and-answer session , participants pressed panellists on South Africa 's continued export of coal and other strategic commodities to Israel , arguing that legal commitments lose force if material supply chains remain intact . <p> Pandor acknowledged the point , noting that coal was precisely the kind of pressure point where activism and organised labour had historically forced political change and said she had been engaging trade unions on the issue . <p> The scale of that contradiction is stark . In the three months to October , South Africa shipped just over 660 000 tonnes of coal to Israel , enough to fill roughly six large bulk carriers , marking the highest quarterly total since 2017 . <p> In the following three months to November , exports climbed again , rising 87 percent year on year to about 474 000 tonnes , according to South African Revenue Service data . <p> Taken together , the figures point to a rapid consolidation of South Africa 's role @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ other exporters have withdrawn . <p> Analysts tracking seaborne coal flows estimate that South Africa could supply more than half of Israel 's imported coal by 2025 , following Colombia 's decision to halt exports earlier this year . The surge has been independently corroborated by industry data and international reporting . <p> These figures became publicly available in mid-December , shortly after the Hague Group 's Cape Town meeting , lending force to the critique raised in the room . They underline the contradiction between South Africa 's role as a legal challenger at the ICJ and its position as a growing energy supplier to Israel at a moment when Gaza 's infrastructure has been systematically destroyed . <p> Pandor 's argument extended beyond coal . Drawing on her experience in government , she described an international environment marked by declining political morality , where economic imperatives routinely override commitments to human rights . <p> Governments , trade unions and even civil society organisations , she warned , risk becoming hostages to the pursuit of prosperity , losing their attachment to justice and dignity in the process . In @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ to speak but to disobey . <p> Her critique turned inward . Pandor expressed alarm at what she described as a retreat from academic freedom , citing partnerships between South African universities and Israeli institutions despite mounting evidence of apartheid and genocide . The erosion of these spaces , she argued , mirrored a broader collapse of progressive resistance that once played a central role in ending apartheid . <p> Other speakers framed Gaza as a test case for the global order . Yara Hawari , co-director of Al-Shabaka , the Palestinian Policy Network , situated the genocide in Gaza within a broader struggle against entrenched systems of domination , argued that Palestinian liberation can not be separated from global movements resisting colonialism , racial capitalism and authoritarian power . <p> In her account , Gaza is not an isolated site of violence but a warning of how repression , surveillance and collective punishment are normalised and exported . That framing , she suggested , makes solidarity and coordinated action across borders not symbolic but necessary , reinforcing the argument that state-level initiatives such as the Hague Group must be @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ force . <p> Saleh Hijazi of the Palestinian BDS National Committee framed the moment as a test of whether the " global majority " was willing to translate mobilisation into material obstruction . He argued that popular action had already shifted public discourse , but that the next phase required backing the Hague Group 's commitments to block the routes that sustain Israeli power . <p> That meant denying territorial waters , ports and borders for the transport of weapons , explosives and energy to Israel , even where no direct arms trade exists . <p> Hijazi called for coordinated pressure to disrupt key transit corridors , from the Mediterranean to strategic maritime passages in southeast Asia , and to sever both direct and indirect links of complicity , including public contracts , academic partnerships and municipal ties . <p> When powerful states attempt to normalise impunity , he said , the response must be collective pushback . The Hague Group , Hijazi argued , represents the most strategic state-level formation to emerge at this moment , but its success depends on sustained mobilisation to ensure that commitments move from @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the Department of International Relations and Cooperation , framed the Hague Group as a response to the systematic erosion of international law through selective enforcement and political exceptionalism . <p> He said the group was established to confront Israel 's actions in Gaza , which South Africa regards as among the most serious breaches of international law in recent history , but also to address a broader pattern in which powerful states evade accountability . Dangor warned that sustained political attacks on both the International Court of Justice and the International Criminal Court risk hollowing out institutions created after the second world war to ensure that all states are bound by the same rules . He drew a distinction between the ICC , which states may opt out of , and the ICJ , whose jurisdiction applies to all UN members , noting that efforts to undermine the court followed directly from attempts by states such as South Africa to hold more powerful actors to account . <p> The Hague Group , he argued , seeks to defend the integrity of the international legal system itself , insisting that accountability @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ from scrutiny by power or privilege . <p> The question of popular mobilisation recurred throughout the meeting . Several speakers lamented the decline of sustained mass protest since the announcement of a so-called ceasefire in Gaza , arguing that the illusion of resolution had dulled urgency even as violence continued . <p> Pandor drew a direct parallel with South Africa 's liberation struggle , noting that apartheid ended not through episodic outrage but decades of relentless pressure . <p> The Hague Group 's emergence reflects a recognition that the old order is failing . Whether it becomes a vehicle for genuine enforcement or another forum for moral posturing will depend on whether its members are willing to align their economic practices with their legal claims . As the coal figures now circulating make clear , that reckoning has already arrived .

@@132627176 <h> SA 2025 : Scenic route from G20 to NGC <p> Going nowhere : President Cyril Ramaphosa had once again weathered an internal push to unseat him before the end of his term in 2027 . Photo : Delwyn Verasamy <p> This was the year that was -- South Africa 's chequered 2025 , a year that ends not with resolution , but with reckoning . <p> As the political elite retreat into festive excess -- sumptuous meals , premium liquor and Cuban cigars -- the country limps toward year-end burdened by unresolved crises and looming choices . <p> Soon after the celebrations fade , the semi-ruling African National Congress will once again face the nation on January 8 , offering its annual statement of intent -- a ritualised blueprint projecting confidence into 2026 and beyond . <p> Yet beneath the rhetoric lies a harsher reality : grinding poverty , entrenched unemployment , fragile governance and a population of 64 million increasingly impatient with political symbolism unaccompanied by service delivery . <p> Looking back from one January 8 Statement to the next , the elephants in the room have multiplied @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ far South Africa has drifted from political certainty , ideological coherence and diplomatic comfort . <h> A political order in fragmentation <p> One of the most consequential developments of the year was the effective collapse of the historic tripartite alliance . The South African Communist Party 's decision to contest elections independently was less an act of renewal than an admission of irrelevance . <p> Analysts were quick to describe the move as a lame-duck mission , disconnected from an electorate no longer animated by ideological nostalgia but by immediate needs : water , electricity , healthcare , housing , roads and jobs . <p> For voters , particularly the young , jobless graduates and the urban poor , the language of struggle has lost its mobilising power . Performance , not pedigree , now shapes political loyalty . <p> The festive season offered no respite from this reality -- especially in KwaZulu-Natal , where political instability once again spilled into public spectacle . A chaotic session of the provincial legislature in Pietermaritzburg , broadcast to a weary nation preparing for holiday shutdown , revealed how brittle democratic norms have @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ row over a no-confidence motion against IFP Premier Thami Ntuli , which he survived , descended into disorder after the Speaker refused a secret ballot . <p> The instigator was the uMkhonto weSizwe Party , now the largest bloc in the legislature with 37 of 80 seats -- flexing its ambition to govern a province once dubbed the " killing fields " of ANC-IFP violence . The symbolism was unsettling : old ghosts , new players , familiar instability . <h> Governance failures in plain sight <p> Elsewhere in the province , tragedy laid bare the cost of institutional decay . The collapse of a Hindu temple construction project in Verulam , killing five people including community leaders , shocked the nation . Municipal authorities confirmed that the project had not been approved . Comparisons were quickly drawn with the George building collapse , marking this as one of the country 's worst construction-related disasters . <p> Even as MECs , MPs and ministers jostled for airtime , the underlying message was clear : state capacity has eroded to the point where basic compliance , oversight and safety can no @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ was arrested with 500 fake passports and large sums of cash allegedly destined for corrupt Home Affairs officials -- a reminder that porous borders , corruption , human trafficking and organised crime continue to intersect with alarming ease . <h> Ramaphosa 's survival as strategy <p> At the centre of this unsettled terrain stands President Cyril Ramaphosa -- a study in political survival . <p> By mid-year , Ramaphosa had once again weathered an internal push to unseat him before the end of his term in 2027 . <p> Deputy President Paul Mashatile publicly shut down speculation , insisting the president was " going nowhere " . At the ANC 's National General Council , Ramaphosa dared his critics to confront him openly rather than plot in " dark corners " . <p> His survival reflects mastery of factional management , control of institutional levers and narrative discipline . Cabinet reshuffles were less about renewal than containment -- marginalising rivals while rewarding loyalists just enough to preserve equilibrium . <p> But survival is not success . The Madlanga Commission of Inquiry into political interference in policing , along with parliamentary @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ of accountability . Calls to release interim findings were rebuffed ; only final reports , the presidency insisted , would be made public . <p> The ANC itself has acknowledged long-term decline . Having lost its outright majority in 2024 , it now governs through a Government of National Unity that functions more as a ceasefire than a project of reform . Policy boldness has been sacrificed for coexistence ; urgency diluted by fear of rupture . <h> Diplomatic humiliation -- and recovery <p> Globally , 2025 unfolded as a study in contrasts . <p> The year 's lowest diplomatic point came early , when Pretoria was subjected to an unusually public dressing down from Washington . The trigger was a toxic mix of ideological mistrust , foreign policy divergence and inflammatory claims by Donald Trump -- echoed by AfriForum -- alleging " genocide " against white Afrikaner farmers . <p> The allegations were absurd but the damage was real . They crystallised how far South Africa 's standing with key Western partners has frayed . Moral language without strategic leverage , in a transactional global order , proved costly @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ subsequent engagements only highlighted how fractured Western consensus has become -- and how expendable middle powers can appear . <p> Africa was not an afterthought but the setting . Development finance , debt relief , energy transition and global governance reform were framed through African realities . South Africa rediscovered a role it understands instinctively : bridge-builder in a multipolar world . <p> The upshot was unmistakable -- a diplomatic win that transcended party politics and reaffirmed national capability . <h> The limits of external success <p> But diplomacy can not indefinitely compensate for domestic drift . <p> The G20 success exposed a persistent paradox : South Africa performs best internationally when insulated from its own political noise , yet struggles to replicate that coherence at home . Institutional discipline abroad dissolves into coalition bargaining domestically . <p> The next local government elections , likely in late 2026 or early 2027 , hover in a constitutional grey zone -- a technical uncertainty that mirrors deeper democratic strain . <h> A year of reckoning , not renewal <p> In sum , 2025 was not a year of triumph , but of @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ bruised , doubted , and diplomatically exposed -- and exits reminded that while its power is no longer assumed , it can still be earned . The country no longer enjoys automatic respect abroad , nor patience at home . Influence must now be built through competence , clarity and delivery -- not rhetoric . <p> The G20 showed what is possible . Whether that confidence travels from conference halls to Cabinet and from diplomacy to daily life , remains the unresolved question . <p> However , Pretoria , barred from the Sherpas meeting , will not call on G20 member countries to boycott the USA G20 and each member could condemn SA 's blackballing from the gathering in Miami , Florida in November . <p> South Africa 's diplomatic rupture with Washington in 2025 did not occur in a vacuum . It was the direct consequence of Pretoria 's decision to take Israel to the International Court of Justice , accusing it of committing acts of genocide in Gaza -- a move rooted in international law but freighted with geopolitical consequence . <p> Closer to home , Durban-raised and @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ faced sustained criticism from political parties and civil society over perceived weaknesses in prosecuting high-profile corruption and violent-crime cases , with some openly calling for new leadership at the NPA . Yet she remained proactive and assertive in confronting internal prosecutorial failures , driving the Andrew Chauke inquiry , which she reported to the president , as a test of institutional accountability . The probe has become a high-stakes legal and institutional reckoning -- not only of Batohi 's leadership , but of broader public confidence in South Africa 's embattled prosecutorial system . <p> If 2025 taught South Africa anything , it is that nostalgia is no longer a strategy . Not in global diplomacy , not in domestic politics , and certainly not in governance . <p> Indeed 2025 stripped away illusion . What remains is choice . At the Crossroads , Quo Vadis , Cry , the Beloved Country : A haunting reflection on a nation 's enduring struggles , reminding us that the questions of yesterday still echo in the heart of South Africa today . <p> Marlan Padayachee is a seasoned former political , foreign @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ and is a freelance journalist , photographer and researcher .

@@132660701 <h> POLITICS <h> EFF condemns US sanctions on ICC Judges <p> Sinawo Thambo <p> 21 December 2025 <p> Fighters say this is a brazen attack on judicial independence by a non-party to the Rome Statute <p> EFF condemns US sanctions on ICC Judges <p> 19 December 2025 <p> The Economic Freedom Fighters ( EFF ) condemns the United States government 's latest extension of sanctions against judges of the Intemational Criminal Court ( ICC ) . The Trump administration , which has repeatedly imposed punitive measures against the ICC and its staff , has now targeted two additional ICC judges for doing their duty by upholding international law and allowing the court 's investigation into war crimes in Gaza to proceed . <p> This is a brazen attack on judicial independence and an attempt by a non-state party to the Rome Statute to interfere with an international institution tasked with ending impunity for atrocity crimes . The United States is not a signatory to the Rome Statute , the treaty that established the ICC as a court of last resort for war crimes , crimes against humanity , and genocide @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ dictate terms to the Court and punish judges , investigators , and officials simply for carrying out their mandate . With these latest sanctions , US Secretary of State Marco Rubio justified penalising the judges for voting against Israel 's attempt to halt an ICC probe , and for continuing to uphold arrest warrants issued for Israeli leaders President Netanyahu and his Defence Minister . <p> This assault on the ICC is part of a broader pattern of US behaviour that undermines international law , weakens multilateral institutions , and protects allies from accountability even when credible allegations of grievous violations exist . The United States has consistently used its power at the United Nations Security Council to shield Israel from censure , vetoing resolutions and deploying diplomatic pressure to ensure impunity instead of justice . <p> --&gt; <p> The EFF rejects this behaviour as dangerous , hypocritical , and imperialistic . A global power that refuses to be bound by the very legal frameworks designed to prevent atrocities has no moral or legal basis to undermine institutions that do act within those frameworks . Sanctioning judges for upholding international @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , it is an assault on justice itself , and a signal that powerful states can evade accountability . <p> This conduct has broader implications for global peace and security . By wielding sanctions as an instrument to intimidate and coerce independent judicial bodies , the United States sets a precedent that emboldens those who commit atrocities , weakens the rule of law , and diminishes the prospects for victims of the world 's gravest crimes to see justice done . <p> Under the Trump administration , we have witnessed an increase in aggression , coercive diplomacy , and a reckless expansion of US imperial interests that destabilises the international order and jeopardises the safety of the global majority . <p> The EFF stresses that the ICC 's investigation into Israel 's conduct did not emerge in a vacuum . It arises from the catastrophic scale of Israel 's war on Gaza , which has resulted in the mass killing of civilians , including hundreds of thousands of women and children , the systematic destruction of hospitals , schools , refugee camps , places of worship , and vital infrastructure @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ population . <p> --&gt; <p> Gaza has been reduced to rubble , its people subjected to collective punishment , ethnic cleansing , and conditions that meet the legal and moral threshold of genocide . This violence is not an isolated event , but part of a decades-long history of dispossession , occupation , apartheid , and brutality inflicted upon the Palestinian people by Israel . For years , Palestinians have been denied justice in international forums . The ICC itself has historically demonstrated bias against the Global South , moving swiftly against African leaders while showing extreme reluctance to act against Westem powers and their allies . <p> It has , therefore , taken extraordinary struggle , overwhelming evidence , and global mobilisation to even reach the point where Israeli officials face meaningful scrutiny . That the ICC has finally taken steps to hold Israel accountable marks a rare and necessary departure from its selective application of justice . It signals that the Court , for once , is beginning to function in the manner it was originally intended as an institution that confronts power . <p> The sheer magnitude @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ made continued silence impossible . The destruction in Gaza is so vast , so well-documented , and so morally indefensible that even institutions long paralysed by political pressure can no longer ignore it . It is precisely this shift that has triggered the United States ' fury . For Donald Trump and his administration to move aggressively to undermine the ICC at this moment , and specifically in defence of Israel , exposes the true depth of the United States ' alliance with genocide . <p> The EFF calls on all US allies , and democratic governments , to isolate and chastise the United States for this interference in the work of the ICC on behalf of Israel . The world can not tolerate a system where the powerful decide who is subject to justice and who is above it .

@@132750928 <h> Starvation as a weapon of war : how Ethiopia created a famine in Tigray <p> Famine -- the extreme scarcity of food -- devastated Ethiopia 's Tigray region during and after a two-year war that began in November 2020 . Yet , the famine 's impact is one of the least documented crises of recent years . <p> Despite the enormous scale of suffering and the far-reaching consequences of the 2020-2022 war , there has n't been enough attention paid to all aspects of the disaster , or to aid to enable the region to recover . <p> The famine dimension of the conflict -- how starvation was used as a weapon of war and continues to shape the region today -- has largely failed to garner the global and domestic attention it demands . <p> We have closely followed and extensively written on the Tigray crisis since 2020 as researchers and firsthand witnesses . In a recent article published by the World Peace Foundation -- a research institution focused on understanding and preventing conflict , particularly in Africa -- we argued that the famine in Tigray was deliberately @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , too , one of us examined how the Ethiopian government and its allies created a " zone of invisibility " around the Tigray war . <p> In our view , famine was used as a weapon in a campaign of destruction in Tigray . Our research draws on humanitarian reports , testimonies , satellite imagery and conflict data to reconstruct how deprivation unfolded . We studied who was affected and why global famine detection systems failed to recognise the scale of the crisis . <p> The lack of reliable data caused by government restrictions , international inaction and structural blind spots in global famine monitoring systems hid the scale of one of the deadliest wars of the 21st century . <p> Famine in Tigray was not an outcome of war . It was the result of policies -- a siege , economic blockade and obstruction of aid -- designed to destroy civilian life . <p> Failure to document the famine has serious implications . It distorts global understanding of what happened in Tigray , limits accountability for war crimes and leaves the international community unprepared to respond to future @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the suffering of entire populations is erased from the world 's moral and political map . It also weakens the mechanisms designed to prevent such atrocities elsewhere . <p> As a result , modern famines driven by political violence -- in places like Tigray , Sudan and Gaza -- are missed , downplayed or denied until it is too late . <p> Background : war and siege <p> The Tigray war broke out in November 2020 between the regional leadership , Tigray People 's Liberation Front , and the central government . <p> From the earliest days it was marked by large-scale and systematic destruction and looting of social and economic infrastructures . Industries , farms , irrigation systems , food stocks , crop fields , orchards , food storage facilities and businesses across Tigray were looted and destroyed . <p> Beyond the physical damage , occupying forces actively obstructed farmers from tilling and planting their land . Within six months , this had driven Tigray -- home to about six million people at the time -- into mass starvation . <p> In famines caused by natural calamity and economic @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ often the worst hit . In Tigray , places along roads were devastated because they were accessible to invading forces . <p> The destruction and looting of infrastructure was followed by a siege that lasted more than two years . The Ethiopian government and its allies imposed a full-scale blockade on Tigray . <p> Banks were shut down . The bank accounts of ethnic Tigrayans -- both within the region and nationwide -- were frozen . The movement of people , goods and humanitarian aid to and from the region , and within Tigray , was brought to a near complete halt . <p> Communications and access to the media were shut down . The multilayered siege left Tigray almost entirely cut off from the world . Commercial and humanitarian supplies were deliberately obstructed for most of the period . <p> This resulted in widespread deprivation , suffering and death , much of which has received little documentation or recognition . <p> The conflict is now described by some studies as a genocide and the deadliest conflict of the 21st century . An estimated 800,000 people were killed through massacres @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ 2022 African Union-brokered ceasefire , hunger and deprivation persist , especially in the western zones and areas bordering Eritrea that remain under occupation . Urban populations whose livelihoods and workplaces were destroyed have yet to rebuild . <p> The implications <p> The Tigray famine reveals deep flaws in how global institutions -- including the UN and international humanitarian agencies -- measure and classify famine . <p> The siege prevented humanitarian access and data collection . This made famine difficult to measure and easier to deny . <p> International famine frameworks rely on indicators like market food prices and malnutrition rates among displaced people . They look at crop failure , loss of livestock and disrupted rural livelihoods . Not at urban households , civil servants or small traders -- groups who were among the worst affected in Tigray . They do n't capture urban forms of deprivation , such as banking exclusion or a sudden loss of salary or assets . <p> Agencies ' dependence on official government data led to a system failure . The absence of data was treated as an absence of suffering . <p> Famine in @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ population . The implications go beyond Ethiopia . They expose how global systems remain unfit for documenting or responding to politically induced famine . <p> What must change <p> To prevent future famines from being erased , there needs to be a transformation of global famine detection and humanitarian response systems . <p> First , the UN and humanitarian agencies must reform famine assessment metrics and systems to account for politically induced starvation . They must include urban and middle-income groups in their analyses . This will help identify and expose deliberate starvation strategies early . <p> Second , human rights bodies should investigate famine as an intentional act of war rather than as an unfortunate by-product . This is a step in the direction of holding perpetrators to account for policies such as sieges , blockades and aid obstruction . <p> Third , donor governments and humanitarian organisations must insist on accountability and transparency in their engagement with states that obstruct humanitarian access to populations in need . <p> Finally , scholars and human rights advocacy organisations should continue documenting how famine functions as a tool of genocide . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ scrutiny . In an age of global connectivity , the absence of data should trigger investigation . <p> Failing to learn from Tigray will leave the world just as blind to the next famine . <p> Written by Teklehaymanot G. Weldemichel , Lecturer in Environment and Development , University of Manchester , and Birhan Mezgbo , Academic Researcher , Tufts University .

@@132883571 <h> OPINION <h> The edge of the precipice : The Islamist threat to Jews and civilisation <p> Mike Berger <p> 29 December 2025 <p> Mike Berger writes the balance between self-interested pragmatism and moral values must constantly be monitored and negotiated <p> 29 January 2025 <p> In the middle of the 17th century Hobbes claimed that in the absence of an absolute Monarchy , a Leviathan , Man would descend into a state of nature in which life would be " solitary , poor , nasty , brutal and short " . The Leviathan would be constrained , not by moral scruples but by rational calculation as would the population at large . Failure of either to heed the strictures of reason would indeed precipitate the chaos supposedly represented by a state of nature . <p> He understood that the preconditions for such a covenant would best be met by a relatively small state composed of reasonably like-minded people . Ironically enough Hobbes wrote his book while exiled in Paris during the English Civil War of 1640 to 1651 . <p> Nearly 5 centuries later the entire world is caught @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ multiple theatres of human existence - varying in intensity from region to region and time to time . Reason , it seems , is more complicated than Hobbes imagined and is in thrall to forces and passions beyond our collective ability to regulate easily . <p> I 'm going to focus on one of them : the Islamist threat to Jews and to Western Civilisation . But first some preliminaries . <p> The Global Infrastructure <p> --&gt; <p> The relentless advance of science and technology keeps changing the physical , social , economic , political and informational landscape at a rate and scale beyond our ability to predict or manage . Donne said , " No man is an island entire of itself ; every man is a piece of the continent , a part of the main ... " <p> " Modern dependence has changed the very nature of statehood . A government may control its borders yet remain vulnerable to financial pressure . It may possess an army yet lack control over its digital space . It may hold elections yet remain dependent on external information flows , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ such conditions , sovereignty becomes layered -- legal at the surface , fragile beneath it . " <p> It 's an important article but , in my opinion , it omits one important dimension which I address here . I 'm referring to the vastly enhanced importance of the " Infosphere " in our lives . That 's obvious across the board and on a global scale but we need to drill down specifically to the role it 's playing in driving conflict across the planet . <p> --&gt; <p> This is not a minor uptick in already endemic human violence but a qualitative quantum leap . <p> ' Information ' in the broadest sense has always played a important role in human affairs , specifically conflict . It was turbocharged following the Gutenberg printing press in the mid 15th century . But this has been utterly dwarfed by the immense , and exponentially expanding , digital information space in the last few decades . <p> In 1990 there were virtually zero internet users apart from some specialised research labs , but regular use rose to over 5 billion by @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ 2020 's traffic was over 1014 bytes per annum : that 's over 200 billion a minute . Innumerable platforms like YouTube , Facebook , TikTok , Instagram , X ( Twitter ) , Reddit , Substack , Patreon and many others have sprung up to cater to a wide spectrum of users . <p> In many cases ' legacy media ' and the ' social ' internet feed off each other setting up self-referential circuits of information . These trends directly and indirectly impact our major academic and professional platforms and institutions . Communication channels like WhatsApp and e-mail allow coordination across and within national boundaries <p> --&gt; <p> New classes of communicators have arisen ranging from academic experts , amateur enthusiasts to professional influencers , many of whom depend on clicks and eyeballs for a living . There are virtually zero filters and this enormous space is inhabited by the full spectrum of human types . On many platforms sensationalism , transgressive provocations , tribal triggers and violence draw the clicks ( and dicks ) . <p> This is a fertile infrastructure for professional propagandists and nefarious actors @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ AI . It is being exploited to the full to drive mass movements and to undermine democracies which are especially open to targeted penetration . The basic techniques of propaganda long predate the present but the technological infrastructure of the present radically enhances its the scale and virulence . <p> The New Authoritarians <p> The major practitioners of these dark arts today include the Islamist movement , Russia ( building on the example and techniques of the Soviet Union ) and the post-Marxian social justice movement in the USA . <p> --&gt; <p> Given that all these share authoritarian goals , it is not surprising that they find common cause despite different ideologies . Nor is it unexpected that they attract fascist orientated malcontents from the Right to join Leftist movements which they would normally oppose . <p> It 's a fertile ground for self-interested opportunists and hyper-empaths alike .. Some are simply searching for meaning , validation and inclusion . Ignorance and fear , power and greed all play a part . Women play a leading role in the social justice-victimhood movements . <p> The point is that there @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ manipulation by skilled propagandists . <p> The Islamism Movement - a philosophic diversion <p> Far too many treat fundamentalist Islam , or ' Islamism ' , as simply a terrorist cult , which in important ways it is . But it is more than that and without a deeper understanding we will not effectively confront the many forms it assumes in the modern world . <p> Historically Islam was created in the pre-medieval era in the Middle East in a world characterised by clan and kin , by power and violence , by honour , hierarchy and pervasive conflict . <p> This world had already seen the rise of two major monotheistic faiths long before the appearance of Islam . Anthropologists call such faiths the Big God religions which served psychological , spiritual and political needs . <p> I 'm going focus on a single point here , the role of such narratives in coordinating and motivating large bodies of men in particular , who would otherwise be at each other 's throats . <p> Islam was particularly effective in this regard by holding out the absolute and unquestioned moral @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ authenticity in terms of unquestioned submission of the self to his will . In return for such sacrifice believers would achieve honour on earth and bliss in heaven . <p> Islam divided the world into dar al-Islam and dar al-harb - a world of peace and harmony and a world of conflict . It introduced concepts of Jihad and martyrdom and many other rules and practices regulating society at all levels . This included Sharia concerning the legitimate behaviour of true believers in relation to one another and to unbelievers and opponents . <p> In its fundamentalist form it represents an all-encompassing package of rules and beliefs . <p> The basic writings of Muhammad have been elaborated upon by generations of Islamic scholars resulting in the proliferation of schools and branches , including Sunni and Shia . Many of these wish to come to terms with the challenges of modernity , including the modern concept of political democracy which is alien to Islam <p> Islamism is broader than Jihadism but it reflects more a difference in scope and means rather than goals . The modern Islamist- Jihadist movement , as @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ revolutionary Shi'ite regime in Iran and numerous militant offshoots in Africa and elsewhere , is characterised by extreme militancy and brutality as encapsulated in its core doctrine <p> " Allah is our goal , the Prophet our model , the Qur'an our constitution , jihad our path , and death for the sake of Allah the loftiest of our wishes " <p> In his book ' Heaven on Earth ' Richard Landis , a scholar of Millennialism , has written a foundational text on the human propensity for vast narratives of a binary conflict between good and evil in human destiny . This has also been called Utopianism , Messianism and many other terms reflecting a totalitarian doctrine with a sacred core and final apocalyptic confrontation . Camus wrote in his notebooks " The idea of messianism is at the root of all fanaticism . Messianism in exchange for man ... " <p> Islamism falls into this form of categorical thinking which is incompatible with the sceptical , empirical , open-ended , rational and broadly tolerant ethos at the core of the ' liberal mind ' . In the modern @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ come to represent prime evil , as it had for elements within Christianity and for post-Christian racial ' science ' in Europe <p> In reality such doctrines can be advanced by stealth or force depending on real world conditions , This is central to understanding its extraordinary success in a world far removed from the pre-medieval era in which Islam and its precursors appeared . It clearly represents a fundamental , deep-seated element in human thought which modernity has failed to extinguish . <p> Preparation for Genocide <p> Were it not for the modern forces of globalisation the Islamist movement , and more especially the Jihadist variants , may well have remained a localised disruptive and essentially predatory enterprise causing misery in those territories in which it gains a foothold . <p> But the impact of globalisation and the explosion of the digital information space has allowed it to gain entry into the vulnerable Western democracies and Third World . <p> The current war in the ME was initiated by the brutal assault of 7 Oct 2023 on the kibbutzim bordering Gaza , in which 1200 Israelis were killed , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ A further 250 ranging in age from toddlers to the vey old were taken hostage . <p> This was not simply a spontaneous act of ' resistance ' or innate savagery . It was the initiation of the final act in the extermination of the Jewish homeland , Israel . To understand if better we need to look at the decades of preparation preceding the assault . <p> The final battle plan had two components . One was the battle for the Western mind as a prelude for the military component to follow . <p> The preparation phase was facilitated by the radical social justice , anti-Western currents already sweeping Western democracies . These opened the elite institutions especially , to penetration . At roughly the same time , a flood of immigrants from the ME , North Africa and other Muslim-majority countries provided both the ground troops and intellectual backing for the coming assault on Israel and the West more broadly . <p> The past few decades has seen a steady stream of money , especially Qatari but including other sources like Turkey and Iran and elsewhere , into @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ introducing radical Islamist themes designed to align with the victimhood-oppressor memes already resident in these societies . <p> The campaign was thus a mix of serendipity and meticulous planning using all possible avenues of influence : technological ( notably videos and images ) , the legacy media , politicians , the burgeoning social media , the Universities and personal interactions . <p> The fundamental message was that the Zionists ( read Jews ) were stealing the land of the indigeneous Palestinian population aided and abetted by white supremacist , capitalist-imperialist forces within the West , especially the USA . A complementary version was that Israel in fact dictated USA foreign policy . These two themes could be alternated according to context and audience . <p> It was a highly unlikely thesis to begin with but it played to ancient prejudices around Jewish power , malevolence and money which I have briefly referred tp .
